Morphisms and comorphisms of sites I Double-categories of sites
Olivia Caramello, Axel Osmond

Abstract
We arrange morphisms and comorphisms of sites as the horizontal and vertical cells of a double category of sites; using the formalism of extensions and restrictions of presheaves, we explains how one can define a sheafification double functor from this double category to the quintet double category of Grothendieck topoi. We describe properties of this double functor and recover some classical results of topos theory through a new notion of locally exact square , generalizing exact squares in the presence of topologies. We also describe a 2-comonad on 𝐂𝐚𝐭 {\bf Cat} for which lax morphisms of coalgebras are morphisms of sites and colax morphisms are comorphisms of sites, explaining the arrangement as a double category.



Introduction

Geometric morphisms can be induced either from morphisms or comorphisms of sites, respectively in a contravariant and a covariant way; the first are characterized through a cover-preservation property (aside flatness), the second through a cover-lifting property. As both define a relevant notion of 1-cells between sites, two questions:

-

is there a proper way to mix them altogether in a single categorical structure on sites, and if so, does it help understanding the reason for which we have those twin classes of functors rather than a single one?

-

is it possible to subsume them into a single notion jointly generalizing the cover-preservation and cover-reflection into a single condition?

In this first part, we will explain how morphisms and comorphisms, though they do not compose with each other, can be arranged as the horizontal and vertical 1-cells of a double-category of sites. Relying on the formalism of extension-restriction applied to sieves seen as presheaves, we will then show how the sheaf-topos construction defines a horizontally contravariant, vertically covariant double-functor to the quintet double-category of topoi and discuss a few properties of this double-functor, as its relation to tabulators, or a conjoints-to-companions phenomenon.

Double-categories are a good environment to manipulate one the most expressive gadgets of category theory, the so called
exact squares
introduced by
[
4
]
: those are lax squares whose corresponding extension-restriction square is invertible; in the context of sites, one can speak more generally of
locally exact squares
, those that are sent to an invertible double cell by the sheafification double-functor. After giving an intrinsic characterization of those locally exact squares in terms of
relative cofinality
à la
[
2
]
, will discuss a variety of examples in and recover several classical results of topos theory as local exactness conditions of some suited squares.

In a second part, we will discuss a reason behind this double-categorical presentation. It is known since
[
3
]
that a 2-(co)monad comes with a canonical double-category of strict (co)algebras, together with lax morphisms of (co)algebras as horizontal maps and colax morphisms as vertical maps. Here, we introduce a certain comonad sending a category to its
cofree site
containing all possible filters of sieves. Then one can exhibit sites as coalgebras for the underlying copointed endofunctor, and more crucially, cover-preserving functors as lax-morphisms of coalgebras, and cover-lifting functors as colax-morphisms of coalgebras. Modulo a few adjustments to incorporate flatness, those results give a more conceptual reason for the dichotomy between morphisms and comorphisms of sites.

A second upcoming instalment of this work will be devoted to the second question, namely the unification of morphisms and comorphisms into a single notion, namely
continuous distributors
between sites. Though both those works will share some mood and rely on the formalism of extension and restriction, they go in quite orthogonal directions; moreover, though distributors and double categories often come altogether, the double categorical structure here is
not
of the kind produced by mixing functors and distributors. To emphasize the independence between those two aspects, we chose to split this program accordingly.

1
Extensions, restrictions, morphisms and comorphisms

This first section contains prerequisites about the main protagonists of our story; after recalling some elementary categorical technology that will ease our manipulations, we recall the definitions of sites, morphisms and comorphisms as well as the way they induce geometric morphisms between sheaf topoi.

1.1
Extensions and restrictions

The present work will rely most on the formalism of
left and right extension
and
restrictions
: those are the three adjoint operations induced by a functor at the level of presheaf categories.

1.1.1
Nerve and Yoneda extensions.

Recall that any functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
with
\mathcal{C}
small and
\mathcal{D}
locally small induces a
nerve
functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{f}}

sending
d
in
\mathcal{D}
to the presheaf
\mathcal{D}[f,d]:\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
, which comes equiped together with a 2-cell
\nu_{f}
whose component at
c
is the transformation with component at
c^{\prime}
given as
f_{c^{\prime},c}:\mathcal{C}[c^{\prime},c]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}[f(c^{\prime}),f(c)]
. On the other hand, if
\mathcal{D}
is cocomplete, this nerve functor admits a left adjoint given by the left Kan extension along the Yoneda embedding

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Then one can show that the nerve can also be exhibited as a left Kan extension through the relation

\mathcal{D}(f,1)={\textup{lan}}_{f}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}

1.1.2
Extensions and restrictions along a functor.

First recall that any functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
induces a triple of adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\textup{lext}_{f}
(resp.
\textup{rext}_{f}
) sends a presheaf
X:\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
to its left (resp. right) Kan extension along
f^{\textup{op}}
as depicted below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{f^{\textup{op}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ran}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{f^{\textup{op}}}}

while the restriction functor
\textup{rest}_{f}
sends a presheaf
Y:\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
to the precomposite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

so that in particular for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
one has

\textup{rest}_{f}Y(c)=Y(f(c))

In fact both the extensions and restriction functors can also be constructed formally as follows. For
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
one can construct two possible nerve functors, the left and right nerve constructed respectively as the composite and the extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(1,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{D}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(f,1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{f}}

where
\mathcal{D}(1,f)
sends
c
to the presheaf
\mathcal{D}(1,f(c))=\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(c)}
, while
\mathcal{D}(f,1)
sends
d
in
\mathcal{D}
to the presheaf
\mathcal{D}(f,d):\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
, which is actually the left Kan extension
\mathcal{D}(f,1)={\textup{lan}}_{f}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{D}}
. But now one can compute either the left Kan extension
{\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}D(1,f)
on one side, while one can compute the left Kan extension of
\mathcal{D}(f,1)
one the other side.

Proposition 1.1.3
.

For any functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
the nerve satisfies the following identities

\displaystyle\textup{rest}_{f}

\displaystyle={\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{D}(f,1)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{D}}f,1)

\displaystyle={\textup{lan}}_{\mathcal{D}(1,f)}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}

while the left and right extensions can be computed as the extensions

\textup{lext}_{f}={\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{D}(1,f)\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{rext}_{f}={\textup{ran}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{D}(1,f)

Proof.

The first two expressions of the restriction functor are standards; for the third expression, observe that
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete, so using that
\text{\char 136\relax}_{D}f=\mathcal{D}(1,f)
and applying the nerve expression of
1.1.1
as an extension gives:

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{D}(1,f),1)={\textup{lan}}_{\mathcal{D}(1,f)}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}

For the extensions, observe that
{\textup{lan}}_{\mathcal{D}(1,f)}\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}
is right adjoint to the left extension
{\textup{lan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathcal{D}(1,f)
; hence by uniqueness of the left adjoints we have the expression of the left extension.
∎

1.1.4
Functoriality.

Extensions and restrictions are functorial relative to composition:

\textup{lext}_{g}\textup{lext}_{f}=\textup{lext}_{gf}\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{rest}_{gf}=\textup{rest}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{rext}_{g}\textup{rext}_{f}=\textup{rext}_{gf}

Suppose now one has a globular 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Then the corresponding restriction functors are related as follows:
\phi
induces in a contravariant way a 2-cell between the nerves
\mathcal{D}(\phi,1):\mathcal{D}(g,1)\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}(f,1)
which in turn induces a 2-cell
\textup{rest}_{g}\Rightarrow\textup{rest}_{f}
whose component at a presheaf
Y:\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
is the whiskering
X*\phi^{\textup{op}}:Xg^{\textup{op}}\Rightarrow Xf^{\textup{op}}
.

This 2-cell has a mate
\textup{lext}_{\phi}:\textup{lext}_{f}\Rightarrow\textup{lext}_{g}
(resp.
\textup{rext}_{\phi}:\textup{rext}_{f}\Rightarrow\textup{rext}_{g}
), whose component at a presheaf
X
is the universal 2-cell
{\textup{lan}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}X\Rightarrow{\textup{lan}}_{g^{\textup{op}}}X
(resp.
{\textup{ran}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}X\Rightarrow{\textup{ran}}_{g^{\textup{op}}}X
) obtained from the universal property of the Kan extension from the composite 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lan}}_{g^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\textup{op}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ran}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\textup{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{f}}

1.1.5
The twofold treatment of squares.

Now suppose one has a 2-cell of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

This 2-cell induces the following squares at the level of the extensions and at the level of the restrictions respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{h}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{h}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{h}}

In the following, we are going to consider canonical cross-adjoint squares, as morphisms and comorphisms induce geometric morphisms through extension and restriction functor respectively. There are two possible such cross squares, the one where one takes restriction along
f,h
and extension along
g,k
, and the one where one takes extension along
f,h
and restriction along
g,k
for a same orientation of the 2-cell.

For the first choice, one can construct a canonical 2-cell as the composite on the right

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{k}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}\textup{rest}_{h}\textup{lext}_{h}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{lext}_{h}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{f}\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{lext}_{h}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{f}}

For the other choice, we can construct a canonical 2-cell as the composite on the right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{h}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{rext}_{g}\textup{rest}_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{rext}_{g}\textup{rest}_{f}\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{rext}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{rest}_{h}\textup{rext}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{rext}_{g}\textup{rest}_{g}\textup{rest}_{h}\textup{rext}_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{\phi}}

Remark 1.1.6
.

Concretely, the induced 2-cell
\overline{\phi^{\flat}}:\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}\Rightarrow\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{lext}_{h}
has as component at
X:\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
the 2-cell induced by the universal property of the left Kan extension at the pasting

while the cell
\overline{\phi^{\sharp}}
is dually obtained from the property of the right Kan extension.

1.2
Sieves and sites

The cross-adjoint cells we saw at the end of the previous section will play a crucial role when establishing the double functoriality of the sheaf construction in the next section: we will see that, if one consider
coverages
on the categories and require a correct assortement of cover preservations and reflection for the functors constituting our squares, then the cross-adjoint cells will restrict between the associated sheaf topoi and produce geometric transformations between geometric morphisms.

We first recall generalities about coverages and sites. Those notions can be expressed in several ways, either through
covering families
, or also through
sieves
. In this work we will mostly use the sieve formulation, for its affinity with the extension-restriction formalism.

Definition 1.2.1
.

A
sieve
on an object
c
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is a subobject
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
n or equivalently, a family
S
of arrows with codomain
c
absorbing under precomposition: if
u:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c
is in
S
, then for any
u^{\prime}:c^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime}
the composite
uu^{\prime}:c^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c
is in
S
.

For a family of arrows
S
with common codomain
c
, the sieve
generated
by
S
is the set of maps that factorizes through a map in
S

\overline{S}=\Bigg\{v:d\rightarrow c\mid\exists u:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c\in S\textup{ such that }\hbox to72.68pt{\vbox to47.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.34138pt\lower-23.5506pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.90944pt}{-5.07996pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.79 -7.03)} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Bigg\}

For two sieves
S
,
R
on
c
,
R\leq S
if any arrow in
R
factorizes through an arrow in
S
.

Definition 1.2.2
.

A
site
is a category
\mathcal{C}
together with, for each
c
, a set
J(c)
of sieves on
c
such that for each
u:d\rightarrow c
and any
S\in J(c)
, there is
R\in J(d)
such that for all
v\in R
, one has
uv\in S
. The data of all the sets
J(c)
will be called a
coverage
.

1.2.3
.

This is the most general definition possible; however one often consider the following additional axioms, which facilitate inferences about coverages:

-

maximality
: for each
c
the maximal sieve
\max(c)=\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
generated from
1_{c}
is in
J(c)

-

up-closure
: for a sieve
S\in J(c)
, if
S\leq T
, then
T\in J(c)

-

stability
: for
S\in J(c)
and
u:d\rightarrow c
, the pullback sieve below is in
J(d)
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Bigg\}

-

filteredness
: for
S,R\in J(c)
, then the intersection sieve
S\wedge R
is in
J(c)

-

weak locality
: if
S\in J(c)
and
T\leq S
satisfies that for each
u:b\rightarrow c\in S
,
u^{*}T\in J(b)
, then
T
is in
J(c)

-

locality
: for any sieve
S
over
c
, if the following
J
-localizing sieve
of
S

J/S=\{v:d\rightarrow c\mid v^{*}S\in J(d)\}

is in
J(c)
, then
S
itself has to be in
J(c)
.

-

transitivity
: if
S\in J(c)
and for each
u:b\rightarrow c
in
S
one takes
T_{u}\in J(b)
, then the multicomposite
\bigvee_{u\in S}u\circ T_{u}
is in
J(c)
.

Remark 1.2.4
.

Upclosure and filteredness follow from the conjunction of maximality, stability and locality; from
[
6
]
it is known that reciprocally, maximality, stability and weak locality together with either upclosure or filteredness ensures locality; finally it is well known that in the presence of maximality and stability, locality and transitivity are equivalent.

Definition 1.2.5
.

A
Grothendieck coverage
on
\mathcal{C}
is a coverage
J
that satisfies the maximality axioms and the stability. A
Grothendieck topology
is a Grothendieck coverage that satisfies moreover the locality axioms, hence all the possible axioms of the list above.

Remark 1.2.6
.

In practice, even if a coverage does not satisfies one of those axioms, it is possible to close it into a Grothendieck coverage or a Grothendieck topology by closing it under pullback, maximality and transitivity: this process does not change the notion of sheaves, not the notion of continuity we are going to consider below.

Remark 1.2.7
.

There is an alternative presentation of topologies in terms of cover: a Grothendieck coverage is the data for each
c
of a set
J(c)
of families of arrows
(v_{i}:d_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
such that:

-

for any isomorphism
u:c^{\prime}\simeq c
, the singleton
\{u\}
is in
J(c)

-

for any
v:d\rightarrow c
, the family of pullbacks
(v^{*}v_{i}:v^{*}d_{i}\rightarrow d)_{i\in I}
exists and is in
J(d)
;

-

for any
(v_{i}:d_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J(c)
, if one has for each
i\in I
a sieve
(w_{ij}:d_{ij}\rightarrow d_{i})_{j\in J_{i}}
in
J(d_{i})
, then the family of the composites
(v_{i}w_{ij}:d_{ij}\rightarrow c)_{(i,j)\in\coprod_{i\in I}J_{i}}
is in
J(c)
.

Remark 1.2.8
.

Sites in this work will be considered as
small generated
, that is, generated from a small dense subsite, even when they are large.

Extension and restrictions have a concrete signification for sieves: they correspond to the operations taking images and preimages of covering families. The following computations will play a crucial role in the remaining of this work as well as its sequel.

1.2.9
Extensions and restriction of sieves.

For a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and a sieve
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
on an object
c
of
\mathcal{C}
, the left extension
\textup{lext}_{f}S
is a sieve on
f(c)
and returns at any object
d
the coend

\textup{lext}_{f}(S)(d)=\int^{c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}D(d,f(c^{\prime}))\times S(c^{\prime})

which is exactly the set of arrows factorizing through the image of
S

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{c}}

Those operations on sieves will provide a synthetic way to work with the two main protagonists of our story, the dual classes of functors one can consider between sites, namely morphisms and comorphisms of sites.

1.3
Morphisms of sites

Recall that a functor between sites
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is usually said to be cover-preserving if for any covering sieve
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
in
J(c)
, the sieve generated in
\mathcal{D}
from arrows of the forms
f(u):f(c^{\prime})\rightarrow f(c)
is
K
-covering. Using the extension functors, this rephrases as the following definition:

Definition 1.3.1
.

A functor between sites
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is
cover-preserving
if for any
J
-covering sieve
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
in
J(c)
, the sieve
\textup{lext}_{f}S
is in
K(f(c))
.

In general cover-preservation is combined with flatness to produce a convenient notion of functor between sites, as sole cover preservation does not ensure convenient restriction to categories of sheaves. In the context where one consider sites both as domain and codomain, one must use the most general notion of flatness, as described in
[
10
]
[lemma 4.2]:

Definition 1.3.2
.

A functor between sites
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is said to be
covering-flat
if for any finite diagram
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
and any cone
(v_{i}:d\rightarrow f(c_{i}))_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{D}
, the set of arrows
w:d^{\prime}\rightarrow d
such that there is a cone
(a_{i}:c\rightarrow c_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
together with a factorization as below is
K
-covering.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a_{i})}

Remark 1.3.3
.

Observe in fact this definition only involve the topology on the codomain category.

Definition 1.3.4
.

A
morphism of sites

f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is a functor that is both covering-flat and cover-preserving.

We will denote as
{\bf Sit}^{\flat}
the 2-category of (small generated) sites, morphisms of sites and transformations between them. Observe that there is an inclusion
{\bf Top}^{\textup{op}}\hookrightarrow{\bf Sit}^{\flat}
sending any topos
\mathcal{E}
on the canonical site
(\mathcal{E},J_{can})
, any geometric morphism
f
to its inverse image
f^{*}
, and any geometric
\phi
transformation to its inverse image part
\phi^{*}
.

Definition 1.3.5
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a topos, a functor
(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
is said to be
J
-continuous
if it is flat and defines a morphism of sites into
(\mathcal{E},J_{can})
where
J_{can}
is the canonical topology on
\mathcal{E}
.

1.3.6
Geometric morphism from morphism of sites.

A morphism of sites
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
induces a geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(f)
whose inverse image is constructed as the left Kan extension
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}={\textup{lan}}_{\mathfrak{y}_{(\mathcal{C},J)}}\mathfrak{y}_{(\mathcal{D},K)}f
and comes equiped with a universal invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{y}_{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{y}_{(\mathcal{D},k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{f}\atop\simeq}

Moreover the inverse image part is also related to the left adjoint
\textup{lext}_{f}
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}}

Moreover, the restriction functors
\textup{rest}_{f}
restrict to sheaves as the direct image part

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)_{*}}

Finally by adjunction this produces also an invertible 2-cell relating the inverse image parts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and at the level of the 2-category of Grothendieck topoi a canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}

This defines a pseudofunctor, contravariant in 1-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Sit}^{\flat})^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}}

1.4
Comorphisms of sites

Recall that a functor between sites
f:(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
is usually said to be cover-lifting, or also to be a comorphism of sites, if for any
d
in
\mathcal{D}
and any
J
-covering sieve
S
on
f(d)
, there is a
K
-covering sieve
R
such that
f(v)
is in
S
for all
u\in R
. Again, this can be rephrased, this time with the restriction functor:

Definition 1.4.1
.

A functor between sites
f:(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
is
cover-lifting

if for any
d
in
\mathcal{D}
and any
J
-covering sieve
S
on
f(d)
, the restricted sieve
f^{-1}(S)
on
d
is
K
-covering.

Remark 1.4.2
.

Equivalently, this conditions amounts to
S
containing a sieve
\textup{lext}_{f}R
for any
K
-covering sieve
R
on
d
.

We will denote as
{\bf Sit}^{\sharp}
the 2-category of sites, comorphisms of sites and transformations between them.

1.4.3
Geometric morphisms from comorphisms of sites.

Here is the process from
[
2
]
[Section 3.3] from which a comorphism of site induces a geometric morphism. For a comorphism of site
F:(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
, denote as
A_{F}:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}
the composite
\mathfrak{a}_{K}\mathcal{C}(F,1)
. Then
A_{F}
is a
J
-continuous flat functor, and it induces a geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(A_{F})=C_{F}:\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}
with
C_{F}^{*}=Sh(A_{F})^{*}={\textup{lan}}_{\mathfrak{y}_{(\mathcal{C},J)}}A_{F}
. In fact, following
[
5
]
[C2.3.18], the inverse image part is also obtained as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{F}^{*}}

More generally a functor
F:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
defines a comorphism of sites
(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
if and only if the restriction functor fits into a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{F}^{*}}

Such a 2-cell has a mate relating the direct image part with the right extension functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{i}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{F*}}

This defines a pseudofunctor, contravariant in 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Sit}^{\sharp})^{\textup{co}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

Remark 1.4.4
.

Beware that this construction is not fully faithful relative to transformations of comorphisms, as the sheafification functor
\mathfrak{a}_{K}
is not fully faithful.

2
Double category of sites

As announced above, our main goal in this work will be to reconciliate morphisms and comorphisms and make them live in a same two dimensional categorical structure on sites. The problem to arrange them into a single 2-category is that morphisms and comorphisms do not compose with each other: of course, one could compose the underlying functors, but then cover-preservation and cover-lifting may be lost. While in the sequel of this paper we will see that a common refinement of those two properties will provide a suitable, composition-stable notion of 1-cell for a 2-category, we here rather
avoid
the question of composing morphisms and comorphisms altogether: this is the purpose of arranging them as the vertical and horizontal cells of a
double category
of sites.

2.1
Prerequisites on double categories

Double categories were initially introduced for a quite different kind of situations where one would consider a notion of maps-like 1-cells together with a more general notion of relation-like 1-cells whose composition might be less strict that composition of maps. Hence this purposeful dichotomy between two class of 1-cells that will not be required to compose with each other, and will be rather related to square-like cells intertwining two possible assortments of such cells.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
double category

\mathbb{D}
is the data of

-

a class of
objects

C,D...

-

a class of
horizontal 1-cells

f:C\rightarrow D

-

a class of
vertical 1-cells

F:C{\mathrel{\to}}D

-

a class of
double cells
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

subject to the following axioms:

-

each object
C
admits both a horizontal and vertical identity
1_{C}
and
\textup{id}_{C}

-

horizontal arrows from a category with identities given by horizontal identities

-

vertical arrows from a category with identities given by vertical identities

-

double cells paste vertically and horizontally

\hbox to87.03pt{\vbox to88.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.51558pt\lower-45.4162pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Definition 2.1.2
.

A
double functor
between double categories
\mathbb{F}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}
is the data of

-

a function
F:\textup{Ob}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\textup{Ob}_{\mathbb{D}}
at the level of objects

-

a horizontal component
h\mathbb{F}:h\mathbb{C}\rightarrow h\mathbb{D}

-

a vertical component
v\mathbb{F}:v\mathbb{C}\rightarrow v\mathbb{D}

-

and for every double cell in
\mathbb{C}
a double cell in
\mathbb{D}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\mathbb{F}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\mathbb{F}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\mathbb{F}K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\mathbb{F}g}

which are moreover to the suited coherences relative to pasting and identities.

In those axioms, we see that composites of vertical and horizontal map, in any of the two possible order, exist only formally without any operation returning a composite 1-cell: only squares intertwining such formal composites are considered, as well as their different pasting.

Example 2.1.3
.

The ur-example is the double category
{\bf Dist}
whose

-

objects are categories

-

horizontal maps are functors

-

vertical maps are
distributors
(a.k.a.
profunctors
)

-

double cells are natural transformations

However the example we are going to consider are quite different and do not share this relational flavour; in particular they wont be instance of
equipments
, an umbrella term describing the kind of relational double categories involving distributors-like notions as horizontal cells. In such double categories, there is a certain dissymmetry between the two classes of 1-cells, one being more general than the other, the other embedding in the second one in the same way as functors canonically define adjoint pairs of distributors. A contrario, in our case, we will consider a more symmetric kind of double category where the horizontal and vertical cells are more like two dual classes of functors, without one being more "relational" than the other. In the last section, we will describe a peculiar flavour of double categories our example of interest will fit in.

But there is a simpler example:

Example 2.1.4
.

For any 2-category
\mathcal{K}
, there is a
lax quintet
(resp. oplax quintet) double category
\mathcal{K}^{\Box}_{\bf lax}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}^{\Box}_{\bf oplax}
) whose objects are those of
\mathcal{K}
, vertical and horizontal morphisms are any morphisms in
\mathcal{K}
, and double cells are lax (resp. oplax) squares. In fact
\mathcal{K}^{\Box}_{\bf lax}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\Box}_{\bf oplax}
are the same thing up to a transposition duality.

2.2
Double categories of sites, sheafification as a double functor

We saw there was two possible 2-categories of sites
{\bf Sit}^{\flat}
and
{\bf Sit}^{\sharp}
depending whether we considered morphisms or comorphisms as 1-cells. Here we exhibit those two 2-categories respectively as the horizontal and vertical components of a double category, on which sheafification will behave as a double functor to the quintet double category of topoi.

Definition 2.2.1
.

We define the double category
{\bf Sit}_{\bf lax}^{\natural}
as having as objects (small generated) sites, as horizontal arrows morphisms of sites, as vertical arrows comorphisms of sites, and as a double cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

a 2-cell of the admissible form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Dually we can define the double category
{\bf Sit}_{\bf oplax}^{\natural}
as having the same data except that a double cell as above corresponds to a 2-cell with the converse orientation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Remark 2.2.2
.

Beware that those two double categories are not equivalent nor related in a simple way: one has as double cells transformation going from a composite of a morphisms followed by a morphism to the composite of a comorphism followed by a morphism, while in the other double category this is the other way around.

Now recall that Grothendieck topoi form a bicategory, so we can consider the quintet double category:

Definition 2.2.3
.

In the following we will denote as
{\bf GTop}^{\square}_{\bf lax}
the quintet double category of Grothendieck topoi, where both horizontal and vertical cells are geometric morphisms, and whose double cells are any lax square filled by a geometric transformation.

Remark 2.2.4

(
[
8
]
[Example 3.4])
.

There is another double category of Grothendieck topoi
{\bf Top}^{\bf Lex}
where the horizontal cells are geometric morphisms, vertical cells are lex functors, and a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

is the same as a natural transformation
\phi^{\flat}:h^{*}K\Rightarrow Gf^{*}
as visualized below in the 2-category of lex functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}

However this is not the same double categorical structure, though the double category of quintets embedds in a horizontally contravariant way in this one. We will only focus on the quintet double category in this part.

We want to construct a sheafification double functor whose horizontal component is the pseudofunctor
{\bf Sh}
form morphisms and whose vertical component is the pseudofunctor
C
for comorphisms. This requires to understand how to manage double cells. Suppose now one has four sites
(\mathcal{A},M)
,
(\mathcal{B},L)
,
(\mathcal{C},J)
and
(\mathcal{D},K)
, related through a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
f,h
are morphisms of sites and
G,K
comorphisms of sites respectively. Then from
1.1.5
we have a canonical cross 2-cell
\overline{\phi}:\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}\Rightarrow\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{lext}_{h}
between the presheaf categories. By commutation of the inverse image, both
\textup{lext}_{f}
and
\textup{lext}_{h}
lift through the sheafifications functors to
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
and
{\bf Sh}(h)^{*}
respectively, and the same is true for the associated
\textup{rest}_{G}
and
\textup{rest}_{K}
which lift to the inverse image parts
C_{G}^{*}
and
C_{H}^{*}
respectively. Hence the sheafification along
\mathfrak{a}_{L}:\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}
produces a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(h)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{K}^{*}}

This is a 2-cell between geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(h)}

Theorem 2.2.5
.

One has a horizontal contravariant, vertically covariant double functor and join full-on-objects-embeddings of the categories of sites with morphisms and comorphisms as the horizontal and vertical categories respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Sit}^{\flat})^{\textup{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Sit}_{\bf lax}^{\natural}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\bf Sit}^{\sharp})^{\textup{co}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf Top}^{\square}_{\bf lax}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

2.2.6
The problem with oplax cells.

We were able to manage lax squares as double cells; but there is an assymetry in topos theory, which is that for a morphism of site, only
\textup{lext}_{f}
and
\textup{rest}_{f}
are visible in the induced geometric morphism, while for a comorphism, we retain only
\textup{rest}_{G}
and
\textup{rext}_{G}
: but in the first case,
\textup{rext}_{f}
is not part of the induced geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(f)
(that is, does not restrict between the categories of sheaves, as in general
{\bf Sh}(f)_{*}
will not have a further right adjoint) while in the second case,
\textup{lext}_{G}
is not part of
C_{G}
, as the inverse image part
C_{G}^{*}
may not have a further left adjoint.

As a consequence, we cannot expect to construct in the same way a sheafification double functor from
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf oplax}
, with oplax squares rather than lax squares as double cells: indeed, an oplax square as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

induces the following Beck-Chevalley 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\flat}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rext}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}^{\sharp}}

and in both case either the vertical or horizontal components are not part of an induced geometric morphism in general.

2.3
Locally exact squares

One could ask what are the lax (resp. oplax) squares that get inverted by the sheafification double functors: this would be a generalization of the notion of exact squares, relative to a choice of topology. This question relates to the characterization of
exact squares
, which we will recall below.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Recall that a lax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is said to be
exact
(or satisfies
Beck-Chevalley condition
) if the associated 2-cell below is invertible

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{k}}

2.3.2
.

There are several equivalent formulations of this condition. First, using the description of
\overline{\phi}
given at
remark

1.1.6
, this means that for each presheaf
X:\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
the precomposite along
k^{\textup{op}}
of the left Kan extension of
X
along
g^{\textup{op}}
is exhibited as the left Kan extension of
X\circ g^{\textup{op}}
along
f^{\textup{op}}
:

({\textup{lan}}_{h^{\textup{op}}}X)\circ g^{\textup{op}}\simeq{\textup{lan}}_{f^{\textup{op}}}X\circ g^{\textup{op}}

In other words, that pasting the opposite square of
\phi
above the extension of a presheaf returns the extension of the restricted presheaf.

From
[
4
]
, we know that the condition of being exact also amounts to the condition that the following induced relation between the representable distributors

\mathcal{B}(1,f)\otimes\mathcal{C}(g,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(k,h)

is invertible; more explicitly the component of this transformation at
(b,c)
and
a
is given by

\mathcal{B}(b,f(a))\otimes\mathcal{C}(g(a),c)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(k(b),h(c))

sending a pair
(u:b\rightarrow f(a),v:g(a)\rightarrow c)
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(v)}

and an arrow
((s,t):u\rightarrow u^{\prime},(z,w):v\rightarrow v^{\prime})
to the composite square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(u^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(v^{\prime})}

Indeed, by the coend formula of composite of distributors, this means that at any
(b,c)
we have an isomorphism

\int^{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}[b,f(a)]\times\mathcal{C}[g(a),c]\simeq\mathcal{B}[k(b),h(c)]

but we recognize in this coend formula the expression of the composite
\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{g}

while on the other hand, the distributor
\mathcal{D}[k,h]
decomposes as a composites

\mathcal{D}[k,h]\simeq\mathcal{D}[k,1]\otimes\mathcal{D}[1,h]

in which again we recognize through its coend formulation the composite
\textup{rest}_{k}\textup{lext}_{h}
.

From this observation, as explained in
[
4
]
, exactness of
\phi
amounts to asking that:

-

for any
u:k(b)\rightarrow h(c)
there is a pair of arrows
s:k(b)\rightarrow kf(a)
,
t:hg(a)\rightarrow h(c)
such that one has a twisted decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(t)}

-

and any such two decompositions are related by a zigzag: if
(s,a,t)
and
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime},t^{\prime})
are two twisted decompositions of a same arrow
u
, then there is a zigzag relating
a
and
a^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
with intermediate decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kf(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(t^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(s^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(t)}

Equivalently, by applying the graph construction of the distributors above, one can see that
\phi
is exact if and only if the comparison functor below is final

g\downarrow c\rightarrow k\downarrow hc

If we now consider a square relating morphisms and comorphisms between sites, it may happen that the natural transformation
\widetilde{\phi}
is not yet invertible in the presheaf category, but becomes invertible after sheafification: such will be the double cells of
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
that are inverted by the double functor
{\bf Sh}
.

Definition 2.3.3
.

A lax square as below between sites (underlying a double cell of
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

will be said
locally exact
if the corresponding transformation below is invertible

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(h)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{K}^{*}}

To characterize such cells, we are going to refer to the following result from
[
2
]
which characterizes morphisms of diagrams into a sites which induce an isomorphism between the corresponding colimits in the sheaf topos:

Proposition 2.3.4

(
[
2
]
[Lemma 2.21])
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},L)
be a site and together wiht a 2-cell as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

then the comparison functor in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\mathcal{X}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\mathfrak{a}_{L}\text{\char 136\relax}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{{\textup{colim}}}\;\mathfrak{a}_{L}\text{\char 136\relax}H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\xi}}

is an isomorphism if and only if we have the following two conditions:

-

for any
u:b\rightarrow H(y)
there is a
L
-cover
(s_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow b)_{i\in I}
together with a family of arrows
(t_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow F(x_{i}))_{i\in I}
such that for each
i\in I
the composites below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(x_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{HG(x_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H(y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{x_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

are in the same component of the comma
b\downarrow H

-

for any span as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(x^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}

such that the composites
\xi_{x}v
and
\xi_{x^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
are the the same connected component of
b\downarrow H
, there is a
L
-covering family
(s_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow b)_{i\in I}
such that at any
i
the composites
us_{i}
,
u^{\prime}s_{i}
are in the same connected component of
b_{i}\downarrow F
.

Definition 2.3.5
.

We will say that a morphism of diagram
(G,\xi)
satisfying the condition of
proposition

2.3.4
is
relatively
L
-cofinal

Proposition 2.3.6
.

A lax square
\phi
between sites as above is locally exact if and only if, for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, the morphism of diagrams below is relatively
L
-cofinal (where
L
is the topology on
\mathcal{B}
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\downarrow c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\downarrow h(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\downarrow c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\pi^{G}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof.

To show that the comparison map
\mathfrak{a}_{L}\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}:\mathfrak{a}_{L}\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{G}\Rightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{L}\textup{rest}_{K}\textup{lext}_{h}
is a natural isomorphism of sheaves, it suffices by denseness of
\mathcal{C}
to prove that at any
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, the comparison map in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
at the representable
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}}:\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{G}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})\Rightarrow\textup{rest}_{K}\textup{lext}_{h}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})
is sent to an isomorphism after sheafification. But by computation of left Kan extension, the presheaf
\textup{lext}_{f}\textup{rest}_{G}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})
is exhibited as the colimit in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
of the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\downarrow c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{G}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

On the other hand, the Kan extension of a representable is again representable

\textup{lext}_{h}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})\simeq\mathcal{D}[1,h(c)]

so we recognize
\textup{rest}_{K}\textup{lext}_{h}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})=\mathcal{D}[K,h(c)]
, which is the colimit in
\widehat{B}
of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K\downarrow h(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{K}}

Finally observe that the comparison map
\widetilde{\phi}^{\flat}_{\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}}
is exhibited as the comparison map between the colimits in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
induced by the morphism of diagram given by
\phi\downarrow c:G\downarrow c\rightarrow K\downarrow h(c)
sending
u:G(a)\rightarrow c
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Kf(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hG(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(u)}

so that by
proposition

2.3.4
, it is sent in
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{L}
to an isomorphism iff and only if
\phi\downarrow c
is relatively
L
-cofinal.
∎

Corollary 2.3.7
.

A double cell
\phi
of
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
is inverted by the double functor
{\bf Sh}
if and only if the underlying square is locally exact.

In the following we discuss a few examples of locally exact squares, recovering in some cases classical results of topos theory as such a local exactness condition for some suited double cell in
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}
.

2.4
Comma and cocomma squares

An archetypical example of exact square is provided by comma squares, whose exactness encapsulates the pointwiseness of Kan extensions, hence its crucial role in formal category theory; it is dually true that cocomma squares are exact. This lead us to ask first what are the correct notion of comma and cocomma in the double category
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}
, which implies to equip them with convenient topologies: they will provide trivial examples of locally exact squares, which are exact even before localization.

Lemma 2.4.1

(
[
4
]
[example 1.14 (2)])
.

For any
f,G
, the comma square below is exact

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\downarrow f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{G,F}}

This means that we have an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G\downarrow F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\pi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\lambda_{G,F}}\atop\simeq}

Now one may ask for an improved version of this result involving topologies on the categories. We first show below that the comma category from a comorphism of sites to a morphism of sites can be itself equipped with a canonical topology such that the projections inherit the appropriate structure:

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
a morphism of sites and
G:(\mathcal{B},L)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
a comorphism of sites; then there is a topology
J_{G,f}
on
G\downarrow f
that makes
\pi_{0}:G\downarrow f\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
a morphism of site and
\pi_{1}:G\downarrow f\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a comorphism of site so we have a double cell of
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({G\downarrow F},J_{G,f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{B}},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{C}},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{D}},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda_{G,F}}}

Proof.

Take an object of the comma
u:G(b)\rightarrow f(c)
: since
f
is a morphism of site, for any sieve
S
generated by a cover
(\gamma_{i}:c_{j}\rightarrow c)_{j\in I}
in
J(c)
, the (sieve
\textup{lext}_{f}(S)
generated by the) family
(f(\gamma_{j}):f(c_{j})\rightarrow f(c))_{j\in J}
is in
K(f(c))
, and by stability of coverages, so is the pullback sieve
u^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(S)
containing all arrows
v:d\rightarrow G(b)
such that
uv
factorizes through some
f(\gamma_{j})
. But as
G
is a comorphism of sites, there exists some sieve
R
in
L(b)
, generated by some cover
(\beta_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow b)_{i\in I}
such that the image
\textup{lext}_{G}(R)
is in
u^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(S)
(that is,
\textup{rest}_{G}(u^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(S))
is in
L(b)
).

Hence we can define the topology
J_{G,f}
as the topology generated from sieves of the following form with

G\downarrow S=\bigg\{\hbox to105.69pt{\vbox to54.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.26305pt\lower-26.32838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
