Then for any such
G\downarrow S
, the sieve
\textup{lext}_{\pi_{0}}(G\downarrow S)
is
\textup{rest}_{G}(u^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(S))
which is covering by the observation above so that
\pi_{0}
preserves covers, while its projection
\pi_{1}(G\downarrow S)
is contained in
S
as it contains all arrows in
S
through which some composite
uv
with
v\in\textup{rest}_{G}(u^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(S))
factorizes, and it can be used to lift any cover
S
of
f(c)=\pi_{1}(u)
, which makes
\pi_{1}
a comorphism.

To finish, we have to prove that
\pi_{0}
is covering-flat. Let be
(v_{i}:b\rightarrow b_{i})_{i\in I}
a cone in
\mathcal{D}
with
I
finite, together with a specified diagram
(u_{i}:G(b_{i})\rightarrow f(c_{i}))_{i\in I}
in
G\downarrow f
; we must show that the set of all arrows
w:b^{\prime}\rightarrow b
such that there exists a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(t_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}

together with a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}

is
L
-covering. For
f
is covering flat (being a morphism of sites), the set of all arrows
w:d\rightarrow G(b)
such that there is a cone
(a_{i}:c\rightarrow c_{i})_{i\in I}
together with a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(v_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}

is
K
-covering over
G(d)
; but
G
is a comorphism of sites, so the restriction of this sieve along
G
is
L
-covering: but if
w:b^{\prime}\rightarrow b
is in this restriction, then one has a cone
(a_{i}:c\rightarrow c_{i})_{i\in I}
as above together with a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(b_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(v_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}

so that
w
is in the sieve we must show to be covering.
∎

Since the Beck-Chevalley 2-cell
\overline{\lambda_{G,F}}^{\flat}
induced by
\lambda_{G,F}
is already invertible in the presheaf topos
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
by exactness of comma squares, it remains invertible in the sheaf topos and we have:

Corollary 2.4.3
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a morphism and
G:(\mathcal{B},L)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
a comorphism, we have an invertible 2-cell between geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{{G\downarrow F}}_{J_{G,f}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{{\mathcal{B}}}_{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{{\mathcal{C}}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\pi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda_{G,F}}\atop\simeq}

2.4.4
.

The result above applies in particular to the following case: let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
a morphism of site; then the projections
\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}:\mathcal{D}\downarrow f\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}:\mathcal{D}\downarrow f\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
are both comorphism of sites (see
[
2
]
[Theorem 3.16] and
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}
is also a morphism of site and is moreover dense, so we have a double cell of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
K_{f}
is the Giraud topology; then by exactness of the comma square
\lambda_{f}
, we know this square is inverted by sheafification, and as
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}
is dense, is induces an equivalence
\widehat{{G\downarrow F}}_{K_{f}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}
we end up with an invertible quintet, where
\widetilde{\lambda_{f}}
is an invertible geometric transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{{G\downarrow F}}_{K_{f}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\lambda_{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf Sh}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f})\atop\simeq\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}

This quintet is an instance of a
companion cell
, which we will describe in the next subsection.

2.4.5
.

A dual class of exact squares is provided by
cocomma squares
. For two functors
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
,
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, the cocomma
f\uparrow G
is the category defined as follows:

-

it has as objects the disjoint union of the objects of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{D}
, denoted
(0,b)
and
(1,b)

-

it has as morphisms those
(0,v):(0,b)\rightarrow(0,d^{\prime})
with
v:b\rightarrow b^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
, those
(1,w):(1,d)\rightarrow(1,d^{\prime})
for
w:d\rightarrow d^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
, together with the classes of formal composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,g(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,w)}

modulo the zigzag relation equating pastings along naturality squares of
\lambda

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,g(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,g(c^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,v^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,f(u))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,f(u^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,g(u))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,g(u^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,w^{\prime})}

Lemma 2.4.6

(
[
4
]
[example 1.14 (3)] )
.

For any
f
,
G
, the cocomma square below is exact

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\uparrow G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f,G}}

Then the square as below define an invertible 2-cell in
{\bf Top}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{f\uparrow G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\iota_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(\iota_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\lambda_{f,G}}}

Lemma 2.4.7
.

For a morphism of sites
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{B},L)
and a comorphism of sites
G:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
, there is a topology on
f\uparrow G
that makes
\iota_{0}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow f\uparrow G
a comorphism of sites and
\iota_{1}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow f\uparrow G
a morphism of sites so we have a double cell of
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f\uparrow G,J_{f,G})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f,G}}

Proof.

The topology
J_{f,G}
is defined by cases as generated as follows:

-

on
(0,b)
consider as generating sieves thoses
\textup{lext}_{\iota_{0}}(S)
for
S\in L(b)
as well as pullback sieves of the form
(\lambda_{c}a)^{*}R
for
R\in K(G(c))
with
a:b\rightarrow f(c)
in
\mathcal{B}
.

-

on
(1,d)
consider as generating those
\textup{lext}_{\iota_{1}}(R)
for
R\in K(d)
.

First let us show that
\iota_{0}
is a comorphism. We saw there are two types of generating covers over
(0,b)
; for those of the form
\textup{lext}_{\iota_{0}}(S)
,
S
provides trivially a lift; for those of the form
(\lambda_{c}a)^{*}R
, observe that
R\in K(G(c))
, so as
G
is a comorphism of sites, there is some
T\in J(c)
such that
\textup{lext}_{G}(T)\leq R
, and as
f
is a morphism of sites,
\textup{lext}_{f}(T)\in L(f(c))
, so
a^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(T)\in L(b)
, and then we have a lift
\textup{lext}_{\iota_{0}}(a^{*}\textup{lext}_{f}(T))\leq(\lambda_{c}a)^{*}R
. On the other hand
\iota_{1}
clearly sends
K
-covering sieves to generating sieves.

To finish we musht show
\iota_{1}
to be covering flat. Let be a finite diagram
(d_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{D}
together with a cone in
f\uparrow G
given by the classes under zigzag of arrows of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c_{i}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,G(c_{i}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,d_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,v_{i})}

As
f
is covering flat, the set
S
of all
w:b^{\prime}\rightarrow b
such that there is a cone
(a_{i}:c\rightarrow c_{i})
together with a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(a_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}

is
L
-covering; but for any
w
in this sieve
S
, one can consider the composite cone
(v_{i}G(a_{i}):G(c)\rightarrow d_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{D}
and we have a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,G(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,f(c_{i}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,G(c_{i}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,d_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,f(a_{i}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,G(a_{i}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,v_{i}G(a_{i}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{c_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,v_{i})}

exhibiting
(0,w)
as being in the sieve of arrows over
(0,b)
whose composite with the cone
((1,v_{i})\lambda_{c_{i}}(0,u_{i}))_{i\in I}
factorizes through a cone in the range of
\iota_{1}
: hence this sieve contains
\textup{lext}_{\iota_{0}}(S)
which is covering by definition of
J_{f,G}
.
∎

2.5
Conjoints to companions, companions to conjoints

Many categorical notions are encapsulated by the exactness of some square, as listed in
[
4
]
[Examples 1.14]. Combined with our observation regarding sheafifications, such exactness criteria can be used to rephrase known results of topos theory as site-flavoured statements of formal category theory. This matter relates to a curious consequence of the horizontal contravariance of the sheafification functor: it interchanges two classes of double-cells, namely
conjoints
and
companions
squares between
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}
and
{\bf Top}^{\square}_{\bf lax}
. Let us first recall those two notions.

Definition 2.5.1
.

A horizontal cell
f
and a vertical cell
g
are said to be
conjoint
(with
f
being the
right
and
g
being the
left
conjoints) if one has a pair of double cells as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

whose horizontal and vertical pasting satisfy

\epsilon\bullet\eta=1_{G}\hskip 28.45274pt\eta\circ\epsilon=\textup{id}_{f}

where
1_{G}
and
\textup{id}_{f}
are respectively the horizontal and vertical identities of
G
and
f
.

Definition 2.5.2
.

A horizontal cell
f
and a vertical cell
G
are said to be
companions
if one has a pair of double cells as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

whose horizontal and vertical pasting satisfy

\phi\bullet\psi=1_{G}\hskip 28.45274pt\psi\circ\phi=\textup{id}_{f}

In the double category
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}
, conjoint correspond to adjunction between a morphism and a comorphism; but this presentation as square connects it with the following formulation in terms of exact squares:

2.5.3
.

A functor
f
is right adjoint to
G
in
{\bf Cat}
if and only if the following square below is exact

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

as this expresses that one has a natural isomorphism

\textup{lext}_{f}\simeq\textup{rest}_{G}

which in particular, applied to the identity, exhibits
G^{\textup{op}}
as the left Kan extension of
f^{\textup{op}}
, hence
G
as the left adjoint of
f
; this produces also a unit cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

which exhibits
f,G
as a conjoint pair in the double category
{\bf Cat}^{\square}
. If now
f
is a flat functor and we put the trivial topology on
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
, so that
f
is a morphism of sites and
G
a comorphism of sites, then exactness of the double cell above says that the sheafification double functor sends this cell to an (invertible) companion pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\epsilon}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}\atop\simeq}

Corollary 2.5.4
.

A flat functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
has a left adjoint
G
if and only if they both induce the same geometric morphism
C_{G}\simeq{\bf Sh}(f)
.

2.5.5
.

If now we consider a morphism of site
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
, it is known that a left adjoint to
f
must be a comorphism of sites, and by a classical result one can find for instance at
[
5
]
[Scholium C2.5.2], they still induce the same geometric morphism between the associated sheaf topoi, and this rephrases as:

Corollary 2.5.6
.

The sheafification double functor
{\bf Sh}_{\bf lax}
sends conjoints to companions.

But this is not any more an equivalence, as
f
and
G
need not be already adjoint in
{\bf Cat}
to induce the same geometric morphism; this leads to the following notion:

Definition 2.5.7
.

A morphism of site
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
will be said to be
weakly
K
-right adjoint
to a comorphism
G
if there exists a
K
-exact square as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

Proposition 2.5.8
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
be a morphism of sites and
G:(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow(\mathcal{C},J)
a comorphism of sites; if
f
is weakly
K
-right adjoint to
G
then
f
and
G
induce the same geometric morphism
{\bf Sh}(f)\simeq C_{G}:{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{D},K)\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)

2.5.9
.

In the following we examine exactness of another pair of dual squares. For a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, one has two possible identity squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and their corresponding mates are respectively the unit and counit of the
\textup{lext}_{f}\dashv\textup{rest}_{f}
adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

If now we consider two sites
(\mathcal{C},J)
and
(\mathcal{D},K)
such that
f
is both a morphism and a comorphism of sites, then it induces two geometric morphisms
{\bf Sh}(f)
and
C_{f}
, with
C_{f}
left adjoint to
{\bf Sh}(f)
in the 2-category
{\bf Top}
. This allows to recover from simple exactness conditions two dual classes of geometric morphisms.

2.5.10
Fully faithful functors.

It is known that a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is fully faithful if and only if its identity square below is exact, which simply expresses that the universal 2-cell of the left Kan extension is invertible

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{rest}_{f}\textup{lext}_{f}=\textup{id}

If
f
is a flat functor and we equip
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
with the trivial topologies, then
f
is also a comorphism since covers are trivial, which exhibits the identity cell above as a companion double cell in
{\bf Sit}^{\natural}_{\bf lax}
, and the sheafification double functor send it to a conjoint cell in
{\bf GTop}^{\square}_{\bf lax}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Hence the geometric morphism
C_{f}
is exhibited as an internally fully faithful left adjoint to
{\bf Sh}(f)
, and as
\textup{lext}_{f}={\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
itself is fully faithful, this makes
{\bf Sh}(f)
a local geometric morphism with
C_{f}
its universally initial section. Then we recover the following result, which relates to
[
2
]
[Theorem 7.20] stating that a continuous comorphism induces the left adjoint of a local geometric morphism:

Proposition 2.5.11
.

Suppose that
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is both a morphism and a comorphism of sites. Then
{\bf Sh}(f)
is a local geometric morphism if and only if the identity square of
f
below is
J
-locally exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Proof.

From what precedes,
{\bf Sh}(f)
will be right adjoint to
C_{f}
; moreover,
J
-exactness of the square above says that
C_{f}
is a section of
{\bf Sh}(f)
, with
{\bf Sh}(f)^{*}
fully faithful. Hence
{\bf Sh}(f)
is a local geometric morphism with section
C_{f}
.
∎

Remark 2.5.12
.

The condition (iii) of
[
2
]
[7.20] establishes that
C_{f}
is local if and only if
f
is a
J
-fully faithful continuous comorphism of site: following
[
2
]
[definition 5.14] this means that

-

for any pair of maps
u,v:c\rightrightarrows c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
, if
f(u)=f(v)
, then there is a cover
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J(c)
such that for each
i\in I
one has
us_{i}=vs_{i}
;

-

for any arrow
u:f(c)\rightarrow f(c^{\prime})
there is a cover
(s_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
in
J(c)
together with a family of maps
(v_{i}:c_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
such that for each
i\in I
one has
uf(s_{i})=f(vi)
.

On the other hand asking for
J
-exactness of the square above amounts to asking for the following functor to be
J
-cofinal at each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}/c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow f(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f/c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{f}}

In this case, applying
proposition

2.3.4
, we have that

-

for any
s:c^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime}
together with
u:f(c^{\prime})\rightarrow f(c)
, there is a
J
-cover
(s_{i}:c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\rightarrow c^{\prime\prime})_{i\in I}
and a family
(t_{i}:c_{i}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
with
(u_{i}:c^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow c)_{i\in I}
such that the following diagram is in the same connected component of
c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\downarrow\pi_{0}^{f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{i}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{i}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}

-

if one has a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}

equiped with tow arrows
u_{0}:c_{0}^{\prime}\rightarrow c
and
u_{1}:c_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow c
such that the following image commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{0}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(s_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(s_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(u_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(u_{1})}

then there exists a
J
-cover
(u_{i}:c_{i}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime\prime})_{i\in I}
such that one already has a commutation in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime\prime}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}

Then, supposing
J
-exactness, applying the first condition of the
J
-cofinality criterion at the pair
u:f(c^{\prime})\rightarrow f(c)
together with the identity of
c^{\prime}
provide a
J
-cover
(s_{i}:c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\rightarrow c^{\prime})_{i\in I}
together with a family
(t_{i},u_{i})_{i\in I}
such that the maps
s_{i}
and
u_{i}t_{i}
are in the same connected component of
c^{\prime\prime}_{i}\downarrow\pi_{0}^{f}
, which means that they are connected by some zigzag

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{i}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{i}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}

made of arrows in the range of
\pi^{f}_{0}
, so that the following pasting in
\mathcal{D}
commutes and ensure an equality of the composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c_{i}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(t_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(s_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(u_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(w_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(v_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Similarly, applying the second condition to a pair of maps
u_{0},u_{1}:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c
such that
f(u_{0})=f(u_{1})
ensures the relative faithfulness condition. For the converse implication, suppose that
f
is
J
-fully faithful; take
s:c^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime}
and
u:f(c^{\prime})\rightarrow f(c)
; by
J
-fullness, there is a
J
-cover of
(s_{i}:c^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow c^{\prime})_{i\in I}
together with
v_{i}:c^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow c
with
uf(s_{i})=f(v_{i})
; so one has to take the pullback sieve induced from
(s_{i})_{i\in I}
along
s
to provide the desired data. Similar argument for the faithfulness condition.

so that in particular
\textup{rest}_{f}
is fully-faithful. This means that
f
is generating in a very strong way, a reason for which such functor are also be said to be
absolutely dense
, and it is known by
[
1
]
that they are the lax epimorphisms in
{\bf Cat}
.

Then if
f
is also flat, seeing it both as a morphism and comorphism of site for the trivial topologies, this means that the induced functor
C_{f}
has an essential image part given by
\textup{lext}_{f}
, which is also lex whenever
f
is flat, and moreover
\textup{rest}_{f}
is exhibited as fully faithful, which makes
C_{f}
a connected geometric morphism. Moreover the dual square of the identity of
f
, through not necessarily exact, provides for a unit cell exhibiting
{\bf Sh}(f)
as a right adjoint of
C_{f}
in
{\bf Top}
, so
C_{f}
is a
totally connected
geometric morphism, with
{\bf Sh}(f)
its terminal section.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Sh}(f)}

Proposition 2.5.14
.

Suppose that
f:(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
is both a morphism and a comorphism of sites. Then
C_{f}
is a totally connected geometric morphism if and only if the identity square of
f
below is
K
-locally exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Proof.

This is because again we have
C_{f}
left adjoint to
{\bf Sh}(f)
in
{\bf Top}
, and now the dual condition of exactness says that
{\bf Sh}(f)
is section of
C_{f}
, and
C_{f}
is connected for
\textup{rest}_{f}
is fully faithful.
∎

Remark 2.5.15
.

Suppose that, for a functor
f
the following square is
K
-exact: then the following functor is relatively
K
-cofinal

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\downarrow d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\pi_{0}^{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

Unfolding this conditions amount to saying that for any arrow
v:d^{\prime}\rightarrow d
, there is a
K
-cover
(s_{i}:d^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow d)
for which the the category of factorization
vs_{i}//f
at each
i
is non empty and connected: this means in some sense that this is
locally
a lax epimorphism.

2.6
Tabulators and other double-categorical constructions

The comma construction can be reformulated in a double categorical manner, providing a special shape of double-limit.

Definition 2.6.1
.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a horizontal cell in a double category
\mathbb{D}
. A
tabulator
for
f
is a double cell of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}

satisfying the (switched) universal property described at
[
9
]
[Definition 3.21].

2.6.2
.

It is known that in a 2-category a comma object with the identity provides a tabulator in the associated double category of quintets. We have the same phenomenon here, though this requires some verification for the double category in question is not simply a double category of quintets but has non-interchangeable vertical and horizontal cells.

The comma 2-cell
\lambda_{f}:\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}\Rightarrow f\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}
above defines a double cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}

Proposition 2.6.3
.

The double cell
\lambda_{f}
is a vertical tabulator for the horizontal morphism
f
.

Proof.

If one has a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

with this time
H
a comorphism of site, then the induced arrow
\langle\phi\rangle
is now a comorphism of site too: indeed for a family
(u_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow b)_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{B}
, if
\langle\phi\rangle_{i\in I}
is
K_{f}
-covering in
\mathcal{D}\downarrow f
, it means that the left component
(Hu_{i}:Hb_{i}\rightarrow Hb)_{i\in I}
are
K
-covering in
\mathcal{D}
: but for
H
is cover reflecting,
(u_{i}:b_{i}\rightarrow b)_{i\in I}
is
L
-covering in
\mathcal{B}
. Hence
\langle\phi\rangle
defines a vertical morphism and we have a decomposition of the double-cell
\phi
in
{\bf Sit}_{\bf lax}^{\natural}
as the pasting of double cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\phi\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\phi\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

We know must check the tetrahedron condition. Suppose one has an equality of double cells as below

that is, an equality of 2-cell between the underlying categories
f*\gamma\psi*h=\phi\gamma
. Then from what precedes
\phi
and
\psi
induce respectively comorphisms of sites
\langle\phi\rangle:(\mathcal{A},M)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})
and
\langle\psi\rangle:(\mathcal{B},L)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})
factorizing universally
\phi
and
\psi
. Moreover the whiskering
\psi*h
defines another functor
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\downarrow f
(though beware, it may not be a comorphism of site for the composite
K^{\prime}h
may not), related to
\langle\phi\rangle
through a universal comparison 2-cell produced by
\gamma
and
\kappa

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}\downarrow f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\phi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\psi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\gamma,\kappa\rangle}

Those data altogether ensure the existence of the desired universal tetrahedron factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{A},M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{B},L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D}\downarrow f,K_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C},J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{D},K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\phi\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\psi\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\gamma,\kappa\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{f}}

∎

3
{\bf Sit}
as a double category of coalgebras

One of the main instances of double categories outside the family of equipments is the double category of (strict) algebras, lax morphisms and colax morphisms of algebra for a 2-monad on a 2-category, as introduced in
[
3
]
[Section 5.4]. By formal duality, we also have for any 2-comonad a double category of coalgebras, lax and colax morphisms of coalgebras. In fact this even makes sense for an arbitrary copointed endo-2-functors for a 2-category, for which the notion of coalgebra is still meaningful. In this section, we describe a 2-comonad on
{\bf Cat}
such that sites are coalgebras at least for the underlying copointed endofunctor, while cover-preserving functors are lax morphisms of coalgebras and cover-lifting functors colax morphisms of coalgebras. This gives an explanation of the dichotomy between morphisms and comorphisms.

3.1
Double category of coalgebras

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a 2-category and
T:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
a pointed endo-2-functor, that is, equipped with a pseudonatural transformation
\varepsilon:T\Rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{K}}
. A
coalgebra
for this pointed endofunctor is the data of a pair
(C,\gamma)
with
C
in
\mathcal{K}
and
\alpha
a section of the pointer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{C}}

A
lax
(resp.
colax
)
morphism
of coalgebras
(C,\gamma)\rightarrow(D,\delta)
is a pair
(f,\phi)
with
f:C\rightarrow D
in
\mathcal{K}
and
\phi
a 2-cell as on the left (resp. on the right)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

such that moreover one has the following coherence condition (resp. its dual one)

Proposition 3.1.2
.

For any copointed endo-2-functor
T
, one can form a double category
T\mbox{-}{\bf coAlg}
of strict coalgebras, lax morphisms as horizontal cells, colax morphisms as vertical cells, and as 2-cell, the lax squares of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C,\gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D,\delta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h,\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\kappa)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Remark 3.1.3
.

The double cells of this double category consists hence in 2-cells
\psi:gh\Rightarrow kf
intertwinning the carriers of the lax and colax morphism structures such that the equality of 2-cell holds

Of course one could also consider oplax squares as double cells, with the dual of this coherence conditions: this mirror the duplication of the category of quintets into the lax and oplax version.

Bare endofunctors are seldom considered without an additional structure of
comonad
; here we chose to dissociate those notions as we will have ton consider objects bearing a coalgebra structure only for the copointed endo-2-functor of some 2-comonad. However, it is worth discussing also the comonadic aspects of the question, for which we recall here the axioms of comonad and the additional structure required on their coalgebra.

Definition 3.1.4
.

A
2-comonad
on a 2-category
\mathcal{K}
is a copointed endo-2-functor
(T,\epsilon)
of
\mathcal{K}
which is additionally endowed with a
comultiplication

\delta:T\Rightarrow TT
satisfying the following strict natural commutations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\epsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\delta}

Definition 3.1.5
.

Let
T
be a 2-comonad on
\mathcal{K}
; a
normal lax
(resp.
normal oplax
) coalgebra
for the comonad
(T,\epsilon,\delta)
is a strict coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
for the underlying copointed endo-2-functor
(T,\epsilon)
equipped with an additional 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

resp.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

satisfying some coherences described for instance in
[
7
]
. A
strict
coalgebra
for
(T,\epsilon,\delta)
will be a coalgebra for
(T,\epsilon)
such that one has a strict commutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Definition 3.1.6
.

A
lax
(resp.
colax
) morphism of coalgebras for the comonad
(T,\epsilon,\delta)
is a lax (resp. colax) morphism of coalgebras
(f,\phi)
for
(T,\epsilon)
such that the following coherence is satisfied

Remark 3.1.7
.

We are going to consider a comonad whose counit is fibered in poset: as a consequence, the eventual lax or oplax coalgebra data will be only statement of an inequality rather than specification of some 2-cell; moreover the distinction between strict and
pseudo
, either for coalgebras or morphism, will vanish for invertible 2-cells in coherence data will be forcibly equalities. Similarly, we will see that the coherences of lax and colax morphisms relative to the comultiplication will become inequality statement that will be trivially satisfied.

3.2
The covers comonad

We introduce here a canonical copointed endo-2-functor on
{\bf Cat}
, which one may see as a
cofree site construction
, sending a category to a category of all possible choices of sets (or filters) of sieves on objects: then sites will be revealed as coalgebras for this endo-2-functor, and morphisms and comorphisms of sites respectively as lax and colax morphisms. We will see moreover that this endo-2-functor actually bears a comonad structure, though sites will only bear a structure of normal lax coalgebra. We will also consider in the last section a relative version of this construction for which this hindrance vanishes and which exhibits some notion of relative sites as coalgebras for the full comonad structure.

3.2.1
Filters of sieves.

Recall that for a Grothendieck coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
, the set
J(c)
of
J
-covering sieves
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
on an object
c
form a poset, which is a subposet
J(c)
of the poset of subobjects
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
. It is a upset as
S\leq S^{\prime}
and
S
covering implies that
S^{\prime}
is covering, and
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
is always covering; moreover, in the case where
J
satisfies the locality axiom, it is even a filter, that is, is closed under intersections.

Definition 3.2.2
.

For each category
\mathcal{C}
and each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
, define
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
the poset of filters of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
that is, is objects are filters
F\hookrightarrow\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}
, non empty-upsets containing the top element
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}

Remark 3.2.3
.

We will say filters for concision, though in the general case of a Grothendieck coverage we do not need to consider intersections of sieves.

3.2.4
.

For a morphism
u:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
, each
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}
defines a pullback sieve
u^{*}S\rightarrow\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
, and this defines a morphism of posets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

If now
F
is a filter of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(c^{\prime})
, the inverse image
(u^{*})^{-1}(F)=\{R\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}\mid u^{*}R\in F\}
is a filter of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}(c)
. Hence we have a morphism of posets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u^{*})^{-1}}

Observe that at the level of subposets (rather than filters) this morphism as a left adjoint
u^{*}[-]
sending a filter (or more generally a subposet)
F\hookrightarrow\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}
to the subposet
\{u^{*}R\mid R\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}\}
of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
. Indeed one has
u^{*}[F^{\prime}]\leq F
as subposets if and only if
F^{\prime}\leq(u^{*})^{-1}(F)
as filters.

3.2.5
The category
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Then define the poset-valued functor
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\bf Pos}
sending
c
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
and a morphism
u:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
to the posets morphism
(u^{*})^{-1}:\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c^{\prime})
. The Grothendieck construction associated to this functor defines an opfibration with posetal fibers

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}

In the following we will denote as
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{C})
the category
\int\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
; its objects are pairs
(c,F)
with
c
an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
F
a filter of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
, while a morphism
(c,F)\rightarrow(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})
is a morphism
c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
satisfying the property that
F^{\prime}\leq(u^{*})^{-1}(F)
– equivalently, that
u^{*}[F^{\prime}]\leq F
which amount to asking that for any
R\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}
one has
u^{*}R\in F
, as visualized by the condition that
u^{*}
restricts between
F^{\prime}
and
F
seen as subposets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

Remark that this defines correctly a category as those arrows composes:
if one has composable pair
u:(c,F)\rightarrow(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})
and
(u^{\prime}:(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})\rightarrow(c^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
, then one has
u:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
F^{\prime}\leq(u^{*})^{-1}(F)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\leq(u^{\prime*})^{-1}(F^{\prime})
so one has
F^{\prime\prime}\leq((u^{\prime}u)^{*})^{-1}(F)
.

Definition 3.2.6
.

We will call the category
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
defined above the
cofree site
over
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 3.2.7
.

One may observe that morphisms in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
differ from the way they are defined in the usual Grothendieck construction for covariant functors. This is because usually one refer by this name to the
lax colimit
of the functor, in which morphisms would be defined in a different ways. Here in fact, our category
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
is the opposite category of the
oplax colimit
of
\mathbb{F}
: indeed a morphism in this oplax colimit
u:(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})\rightarrow(c,F)
is a pasting of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{c\in\mathcal{C}}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\;\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u^{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\leq}}

where
q_{u}:(c^{\prime},(u^{*})^{-1}(F))\rightarrow(c,F)
represents the generic cartesian morphism at
u
while the left cell represent an inequality
F^{\prime}\leq(u^{*})^{-1}(F)
; but this oplax colimit is fibered over
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{op}}
while we need a construction which admits a covariant canonical projection to
\mathcal{C}
itself: hence our choice to consider
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}={\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
, where such data are formally inverted to be represented by a morphism
u:(c,F)\rightarrow(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})
, though the left cell still codes for the same inequality. Hence the discrepancy with the usual Grothendieck construction.

3.2.8
The endofunctor
\mathbb{S}
.

Moreover this construction is functorial on
{\bf Cat}
: for a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, recall that one can define for each sieve
S\rightarrowtail\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
in
\mathcal{C}
the sieve
\textup{lext}_{f}S=\{v:d\rightarrow f(c)\mid\exists u:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c\in S\textup{ such that }v\leq f(u)\}
. For a given filter
F
of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
, one can consider the filter
\textup{lext}_{f}[F]
generated from the set of sieves of the form
\textup{lext}_{f}(S)
for
S
in
F
. This filter contains every sieve
R\rightarrow\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(c)}
that contains a sieve of the form
\textup{lext}_{f}(S)
for
S
in
F
.

Then for a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, define
\mathbb{S}f
as sending
(c,F)
to
(f(c),\textup{lext}_{f}[F])
: then for
u:(c^{\prime},F^{\prime})\rightarrow(c,F)
and
R\in\textup{lext}_{f}[F]
, there is
S\in F^{\prime}
such that
u\circ S\leq R
, hence
f(u)\circ\textup{lext}_{f}(S)\leq\textup{lext}_{f}(R)
and
\textup{lext}_{f}(S)\in\textup{lext}_{f}[F^{\prime}]
, so that
f(u)
defines a map
(f(c^{\prime}),\textup{lext}_{f}[F^{\prime}])\rightarrow(f(c),\textup{lext}_{f}[F])
. Hence by up-closure, for
S\leq u^{*}R
, one has
u^{*}R\in F^{\prime}
, and hence
f(u)^{*}[\textup{lext}_{f}(R)]\in\textup{lext}_{f}[F^{\prime}]
: this ensures that
f(u)^{*}[\textup{lext}_{f}[F]]\leq\textup{lext}_{f}[F^{\prime}]
, so that
f(u)
defines a morphism
(f(c^{\prime}),F^{\prime})\rightarrow(f(c),F)
in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}
, so that
\mathbb{S}f
is a functor.

For a natural transformation
\phi:f\Rightarrow g
, we want a natural transformation
\mathbb{S}\phi:\mathbb{S}f\Rightarrow\mathbb{S}g
, which requires
\mathbb{S}\phi_{(c,F)}:(f(c),\textup{lext}_{f}[F])\rightarrow(g(c),\textup{lext}_{g}[F])
to define a morphism in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}
: let be
S\in\textup{lext}_{g}[F]
; then there is
R
in
F
such that
S
is generated from the
g(v)
with
v\in S
; but then the naturality square of
\phi
at
v:c^{\prime}\rightarrow c
ensures that for any
v\in S
one has
\phi_{c}f(v)=g(v)\phi_{c^{\prime}}
so that
\phi_{c}\circ\textup{lext}_{f}[S]\leq R
.

3.2.9
In term of restrictions.

Seeing a filter
F
of
\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{C}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})
as a poset map
F:\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{C}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})\rightarrow 2
, the direct and inverse images correspond respectively to the following extension and precomposition functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{C}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{D}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(c)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{lext}_{f}[F]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{C}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{D}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(c)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{rest}_{f}^{-1}(F)}

and by generality on extensions along adjoint we know that
{\textup{lan}}_{\textup{lext}_{f}}\simeq\textup{rest}_{f}^{-1}
, that is

\textup{lext}_{f}[F]\simeq(\textup{rest}_{f})^{-1}(F)=\{S\in\textup{ Sub}_{\mathcal{D}}(\text{\char 136\relax}_{f(c)})\mid\textup{rest}_{f}(S)\in F\}

Remark 3.2.10

(More general version)
.

We could have defined alternatively the endo-2-functor
\mathcal{S}
in a slightly more general manner that will fit for non-Grothendieck coverages. For
\mathcal{C}
a category, we could define
\mathbb{S}_{0}\mathcal{C}
as having

-

as objects pairs
(c,F)
with
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
F
a
set
of sieves on
c

-

as morphisms
u:(b,G)\rightarrow(c,F)
morphisms
u:b\rightarrow c
in
\mathcal{C}
such that one has

\forall R\in F,\exists S\in G,\forall v\in S,uv\in R

In this formulation we only require that for any sieve
R
in
F
there is some
S\in G
contained in
u^{*}R
, though we do not explicitly stipulate that
u^{*}R
is in
G
, which is not automatic as
G
is not required to be upclosed. However in our context, it will be more practical to enforce upclosure of the sets of sieves and use explicitely pullback sieves.

3.2.11
Counit and comultiplication.

The endo-2-functor
\mathbb{S}
is copointed through the natural projections which will play the role of counits:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We now want to endow
\mathbb{S}
with a comultiplication structure to make it a comonad. Beforehand we must give a minimal amount of details about the iterated cofree site
\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
: its objects are triples
(c,F,\mathcal{F})
with
(c,F)
in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}
a filter of sieves over
(c,F)
in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
; here a sieve will be simply a downard closed collection of arrows
v:(d,G)\rightarrow(c,F)
, which have to satisfy the condition that
F\leq(v^{*})^{-1}(G)
, that is, that for any sieve
S
over
c
in
F
, the pullback sieve
v^{*}S
is in the filter
G
.

Then there is a canonical way to embed
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
into
\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
: for an object
(c,F)
in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
and
S\in F
, we can define the
stabilizing sieve
in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
as the sieve generated from the stabilization
v:(b,u^{*}[F])\rightarrow(c,F)
, that is,

\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}=\bigg\{v:(d,G)\rightarrow(c,F)\mid\exists u\in S\textup{ such that }\hbox to169.7pt{\vbox to43.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.85133pt\lower-21.81943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Then we define, for
(c,F)
of
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
, the filter generated from stabilization of sieves in
F
:

\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)}=\uparrow\{\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}\mid S\in F\}

in such a way that a sieve on
(c,F)
is in
\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)}
if and only if it contains a stabilizing sieve of the form
\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}
with
S
in
F
.

Lemma 3.2.12
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
, the construction above defines a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}

sending
(c,F)
to
(c,F,\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)})
, that is to its filter of stabilizations.

Proof.

We have to check that a map
v:(d,G)\rightarrow(c,F)
with
v^{*}[F]\leq G
will induce a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,G,\mathcal{F}_{(d,G)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,F,\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

which is to say that
v^{*}[\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)}]\leq\mathcal{F}_{(d,G)}
. It suffices to check that if
S\in F
, then the image of its stabilizing sieve
v^{*}[\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}]
contains a stabilizing sieve of the form
\mathcal{R}_{(R,G)}
for
R\in G
. The sieve
v^{*}[\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}]
is the sieve on

defined as

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{30.97484pt}{-2.1268pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 42.86 -2.94)} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bigg\}

so that in particular, if
w\in v^{*}[\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}]
, then in particular the underlying map
w
is in
v^{*}[S]
for it factorizes through some
u\in S
, and moreover one has
x^{*}[u^{*}[F]]\leq H
. But from the very condition that
v
was a map in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
it is stipulated that
v^{*}[F]\leq G
, so in particular
v^{*}S\in G
, and we can then consider its stabilization
\mathcal{R}_{(v^{*}[S],G)}
, which is then in
\mathcal{F}_{(d,G)}
: but we can see that
\mathcal{R}_{(v^{*}[S],G)}\in v^{*}[\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}]
: indeed, if
w\in v^{*}[S]
, then there is some factorization as below with
u\in S

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\in S}

and from
v^{*}F\leq G
, we have
x^{*}[u^{*}[F]]=w^{*}[v^{*}[F]]\leq w^{*}[G]
so that the square above lifts to a square in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a,w^{*}G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,u^{*}[F])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\in S}

which ensures that
w
is in
v^{*}[\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}]
by its characterization above. This ensures functoriality of the construction
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.13
.

The data of the functors
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
define altogether a natural transformation
\delta:\mathbb{S}\Rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}
.

3.2.14
Iteration of counit.

In the following we are also going to consider the two possible iterations of the free construction at this projection:

-

the functor
\pi_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
sends an object
(c,F,\mathcal{F})
to the underlying
(c,F)

-

the functor
\mathbb{S}\pi_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
sends
(c,F,\mathcal{F})
to the pair
(c,\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F}))
, where
\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F})
is the filter generated from sieve of the form
\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{R})
for
\mathcal{R}
a sieve in
\mathcal{F}
: those image sieve simply are the underlying sieves in
\mathcal{C}
generated from the underlying sieve of
\mathcal{R}
over
c
.

Proposition 3.2.15
.

The data
(\mathbb{S},\pi,\delta)
make
\mathbb{S}
a comonad on
{\bf Cat}
.

Proof.

We have to validate the axioms of comonads. We fix some category
\mathcal{C}
.

-

For the first axioms: the composite
\pi_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
is trivially the identity for the underling object of
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(c,F)
is
(c,F)
itself: so we do have the first retraction condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}

-

For the second axiom, the composite
\mathbb{S}\pi_{\mathcal{C}}\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
sends
(c,F)
to the pair
(c,\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}[\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)}])
; but
\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}[\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)}]
is the filter generated from underlying sieves
\textup{lext}_{\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)})
, for
S\in F
, but
\mathcal{R}_{(S,F)}
is precisely defined as the sieve
S
itself equiped with stabilization of
F
, so its underlying sieve is
S
itself. So we do have the second retraction condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\pi_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}

-

For the last axioms, we compute first
\delta_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}(c,F,\mathcal{F})=((c,F,\mathcal{F}),\mathcal{F}_{(c,F,\mathcal{F})})
where
\mathcal{F}_{(c,F,\mathcal{F})}
is the filter generated from sieves of the form
\langle\{(d,G,v^{*}[\mathcal{F}])\rightarrow(c,F,\mathcal{F})\},v\in\mathcal{R}\rangle
with
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{F}
.
On the other hand,
\mathbb{S}\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(c,F,\mathcal{F})=(c,F,\mathcal{F},\textup{lext}_{\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F}))
, where
\textup{lext}_{\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F})
is the filter generated from sieves of the form
\textup{lext}_{\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{R})
, which are precisely sieves generated from families of the form
\{(d,G,v^{*}[\mathcal{F}])\rightarrow(c,F,\mathcal{F}),v\in\mathcal{R}\}
. Hence applying those composites to
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(c,F)
clearly begets the same value for
\textup{lext}_{\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)})=\mathcal{F}_{((c,F),\mathcal{F}_{(c,F)})}
. This ensures the last condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}

∎

3.3
Sites morphisms and comorphisms as (co)lax morphisms

We established that
\mathbb{S}
is a comonad on
{\bf Cat}
. This comonad sends a category to the category of all possible choices of filter of subobjects over its objects: as coverage and Grothendieck topologies pick exactly such a filter at each object, we can foreseen that they have something to do with coalgebra structures. This is what we discuss in this subsection; as a first step, it is quite easy to see that coverages are the same as sections of the projection maps (forgetting the comultiplication):

Proposition 3.3.1
.

A Grothendieck coverage on
\mathcal{C}
is the same as a coalgebra structure on
\mathcal{C}
for the underlying copointed endo-2-functor
(\mathbb{S},\pi)
.

Proof.

A coalgebra defines a section
J:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}
of
\pi_{\mathcal{C}}
. This means that for any
c
the object
J(c)
is of the form
(c,J(c))
with
J(c)
a filter of
\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
, that is, consist of a collection of sieves on
c
such that
\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}
is in
J(c)
(that is, the maximal sieve
\max(c)
is in
J(c)
), and
J(c)
is up-closed for inclusion. Moreover functoriality says that for any
u:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
, one has a restriction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(c^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{ Sub}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}\text{\char 136\relax}_{c^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

expressing that for any
R
in
J(c^{\prime})
the pullback sieve
u^{*}R
is in
J(c)
. This is exactly what a coverage is.
∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

For the same reason, one can easily see why a general coverage (not necessarily Grothendieck) is a coalgebra structure for the modified version of the cofree site comonad
\mathbb{S}_{0}
described at
remark

3.2.10
.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

For any Grothendieck coverage
(\mathcal{C},J)
, we have at each
c
of
\mathcal{C}
an inequality

\mathcal{F}_{(c,J(c))}\leq\textup{lext}_{J}[J(c)]

In other word, any Grothendieck coverage defines a structure of normal lax coalgebra for the comonad
(\mathcal{S},\pi,\delta)
.

Proof.

If
J
is a coverage, then for any
u:b\rightarrow c
in
\mathcal{C}
, one has
u^{*}[J(c)]\leq J(b)
as for any
S\in J(c)
, the pullback sieve
u^{*}S\in J(b)
, and this exactly says that for any
u\in S
, one has a factorization in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,J(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,J(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,u^{*}[J(c)])}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\in S}

This means that the sieve
\textup{lext}_{J}(S)
is contained in the stabilizing sieve
\mathcal{R}_{(S,J(c))}
, which hence must be in the generated filter
\textup{lext}_{J}[J(c)]
. Hence for any
S\in J(c)
, one has
\mathcal{R}_{(S,J(c))}\in\textup{lext}_{J}[J(c)]
, and as
\mathcal{F}_{(c,J(c))}
is generated from those stabilizing filters, one has the desired inequality.∎

3.3.4
.

The missing coherence condition in
proposition

3.3.1
is the comultiplication condition that the following square commutes strictly

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}J}

which amounts to having at any
c
in
\mathcal{C}
an equality

\mathcal{F}_{(c,J(c))}=\textup{lext}_{J}[J(c)]

We saw at
proposition

3.3.3
that the left to right inequality is always true for a coverage; we show here that the converse inequality is related to transitivity. Asking that
\textup{lext}_{J}[J(c)]\leq\mathcal{F}_{(c,J(c))}
amounts to asking that for any
R\in J(c)
, the sieve
\textup{lext}_{J}(R)
contains a generating sieve of
\mathcal{F}_{(c,J(c))}
, that is, there is a sieve
S\in J(c)
such that
\mathcal{R}_{(S,J(c))\leq}\textup{lext}_{J}(R)
; hence for such a sieve
S
one will have that for any
v:d\rightarrow c
in
S
, there will be a
u:b\rightarrow c
in
R
and a factorization
v=uw
such that one has a factorization in
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d,v^{*}[J(c)])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,J(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(b,J(b))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\in S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\in R}

expressing that
w^{*}[J(b)]\leq v^{*}[J(c)]
on
d
. This later condition says that for any
T\in J(b)
,
w^{*}T\in v^{*}[J(c)]
, which is to say that there is some
Q\in J(c)
such that
v^{*}Q\leq w^{*}T
, that is, that for any
f
on
d
such that
vf
factorizes through some
f^{\prime}\in Q
, there is some
g
such that
wy
factorizes through some
g^{\prime}\in T
and a factorization as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}\in Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\in S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\in T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\in R}

The condition we obtain by unfolding the coherence condition can itself be seen as a local form of transitivity: it says that for any
J
-coviering sieve
R
there is another test
J
-covering sieve
S
such that for any choice of
J
-covering sieves on domains of maps in
R
, the composite sieve will “look" covering locally in
S
. This condition is rather strong and does not seems to be satisfied by Grothendieck coverages nor topologies, as it forces somewhat the filter to be related in a very tight manner through pullback.

At the level of morphisms and comorphisms however there is a good correspondence with morphisms of coalgebras, without complications arising from the comultiplication:

Proposition 3.3.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},J)
,
(\mathcal{D},K)
be coalgebras for
(\mathbb{S},\pi,\delta)
and
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
a functor:

-

f
supports a lax morphism of coalgebras if and only if it is cover preserving;

-

f
supports a colax morphism of coalgebras if and only if it is cover reflecting.

This remains true if
(\mathcal{C},J)
,
(\mathcal{D},K)
are coalgebras for the underlying copointed endofunctor
(\mathbb{S},\pi)
.

Proof.

For the first item, suppose that one has a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

This consists for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
of a morphism
(f(c),K(f(c)))\rightarrow(f(c),\textup{lext}_{f}[J(c)])
; moreover, from the axiom of retraction

\hbox to87.2pt{\vbox to85.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.59718pt\lower-43.79721pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-43.59718pt}{-35.56947pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -60.33 -49.22)} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -20.46 43.22 L 19.33 43.22}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.16945pt}{31.23616pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.61 43.22)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.55038pt}{-24.20692pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.53 -33.5)} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -17.09 -15.74 h 33.89 v 31.48 h -33.89 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.19621pt}{-2.49998pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -14.11 -3.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

we know that the underlying morphism
\pi_{\mathcal{D}}(\phi_{c})
in
\mathcal{D}
is the identity of
f(c)
, so it has to live in the fiber
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{D}}(f(c))
; but as
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}
has posetal fibers, the existence of such a 2-cell amounts to an inequality between subobjects

\textup{lext}_{f}[J(c)]\leq K(f(c))

which means that if a sieve
R
contains the image of a sieve of
J(c)
, then it is in
K(f(c))
. Hence the sieve generated from the arrows of the form
f(u):f(c^{\prime})\rightarrow f(c)
for
u\in S
with
S
in
J(c)
is in particular in
K(f(c))
, which makes
f
a morphism of sites
(\mathcal{C},J)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},K)
.

For the second item, suppose that one has a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

This consists for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
of a morphism
\phi_{c}:(f(c),\textup{lext}_{f}[J(c)])\Rightarrow(f(c),K(f(c)))
, whose underlying map in
\mathcal{D}
is the identity of
f(c)
by the same argument as above: but as
\mathbb{S}\mathcal{D}
has posetal fibers, the existence of such a 2-cell amounts to an inequality between subobjects

K(f(c))\leq\textup{lext}_{f}[J(c)]

which means any sieve
R
in
K(f(c))
contains a sieve of the form
\textup{lext}_{f}S
for some
S
in
J(c)
, so that there is a sieve
S
on
c
such that the sieve generated from the
f(u)
with
u
in
S
is contained in
R
.

To finish, observe that if
(\mathcal{C},J)
,
(\mathcal{D},K)
are coalgebras for the whole comonad structure of
\mathbb{S}
, that is, satisfy the comultiplication axiom, then a structure on
f
of lax or colax morphism for the copointed endofunctor will lift automatically to a structure of lax or colax morphism for the whole comonad: indeed, the counit condition ensures that the structure map of a lax or a colax morphism is an inequality. As there is at most one inequality, one forcibly have the coherence
